=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.97)
No. 65,
AMERICAN CONSULATE
CONFIDENTIAL
Questions of Ir ternat fonal Law and of
Americar Pelicy Raised by Dr. George
SUBJECT:
H. Blakeslee's Article "Japan's Mandated
Islan is" in Derartnort of State BULLETIN.
THE SECRETARY OF STATE.
C
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the article "Japan's
Mandated Islands" by Dr. Georfe H. Blakeslee which
afrears in the Department of State BULLETIN of
December 17, 1944, Vol. XI, No.286, and particularly
to the sentence on page 765 which opens the section
"United States Interests to 19l9" and which reads as
follows:
"Missions from the United States. United
States missionary influence became so
strong throughout the Marshalls and the
eastern Carolines that the claims of the
missions alone would probably have been
ample to justify the United States in
extending its political control over all
the archipelagoes in this part of the
Pacific."
The implication that there is or can be a nexus
between missionary work and political control is so
challenging that a request for further information on
Doctor Blakeslee's statement is respectfully ventured.
Perhaps it may be a superficial observation on my part,
but if any one thing did impress me during a career
in China it was that the Chinese in fact did challenge
Christian missionary work not on its religious or
ethical content but on its political character -
as something altogether too intimately connected with
the political motives of foreign powers. And on the
other hand the missionaries of all nationalities went
through many trying experiences, including martyreem,
to refute the Chinese contention and to persuade the
Chinese that missionary work was based solely en
SEP&01905
JUN 7r

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
religious motives and had no politieal significance.
So much so, that in the American-Chinese treaty of
1903 it was deemed necessary to state in the open-
inf sentence of Article XIv what the principles of
the Christian relipion were, what the Chinese were
intended to understand what American missionary
effort meant.
One recalls the cynicism with which
was met the Japanese demand in 1915 for the right to
ownership of land for the building of Japanese
"hospitals, churches and sehools."
In my own
experience similar cynicism was directed by some
Chinese toward Christian hospitals, churches and
schools.
Neither in the such standard works as Moore's
International Digest under the subject "Sovereignty;
Its Acquisition and Loss"; nor in Hyde's International
Law - Chiefly as Interpreted and Applied by the United
States under the subject "General Rights of Property
and Control"; nor in Hackworth's Digest of International
Law under the heading "Territory and Sovereignty of
States" do I find material which supports Doctor
Blakeslee's proposition that the claims (presence ?
residence ? property ?) of American missions might be
the basis for the extension of American political
control (sovereignty ?).
I do not wish to be understood as challonging
the wisdom of a policy which might have resuljed in
the acquisition by the United States of the territory
referred to by Doctor Blakeslee, but in the light of
the opposition of the Chinese (and perhaps other non-
Christian peoples) for many years to missionary work
precisely because of the political character they
attached thereto (and strenuously denied by the
occidental0 it appears proper to raise the question
whether Doctor Blakeslee's statement is tenable:in
international law or has ever received recegnition by
the United States Government in any premouneements of
national poliey. As a subject for researeh, Doctor
Blakeslee's proposition might well be worth pursuing
in American "Foreign Relations" and in works on inter-
national law.
Respectfully yours
Samuel Bokebin
American Consul
801.3
Ss:mee
In single osalid only to Department.
Cepy to Eabassy, London.

October 2, 1946
General of the Army Douglus LacArthur,
Supreme Commander of the Allied Powers,
Tokyo.
Your Excellency:
In behalf of tne leading Okinawan signatories now
residing in Japan, we have the honor of submitting this
Fetition to Your Excellency for your sympathetic
cousideration.
When the Military Administration wus promulgated
in Okinawa immediately after the occupation by the
American Forces last summer, the Military Government
lost no time in establishing hospitals and infirmaries
at strategic points to give medical care to the afflioted.
in distributing food and clothings to all the people and
in supplying lumber and building materials for the re-
building of homes. For these acts of human kindness, the
people of Okinawa have felt most grateful to the Military
Government and the people of the United States. The
inhabitants of the islands with a few exceptions have
now returned to their towns and villages as though oblivi-
ous of the horrors of the past war and are busily engaged
in the rebuilding of their homes and in the cultivation
of their lands while collaborating whole heartedly with
the authorities there in the rehabilitation of the varied

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)
peace time industries and institutions.
While manifesti.g  deer sense of gratitude and
fully up re juting the generosity of the united states.
the Ckinawans connected as they are in blood with the
people of Japan, are eurnestly craving to be again
placed under the administrutive care of the Japanese
Government and their homeland to be returned to Japan
as and wnen conditions would warrant sucn ciunges.
It is said tnat blosd is thicker than water and our
people fully coglizant of the fact that they are of
the Japunese ruce and wiling to brave wnatever con-
ditions destiny may thrust upon the vanguished nation.
stand ever ready to share in the fate of their con-
sauguineous brothers of Jupan.
In certain uarters of America and Europe, there
seems to exist a fallacious view that the reople of
Japan have in the past looked down upon the Okinawans
as their pour cousins and have  ot, therefore, accorded
them fair and symrathetic treatment. In this connection
it could be said witn impunity that neither the Govern-
ment nor the people have ever ccorded the Okinawans
any discriminatory treatment whatsoever. Okinawa has
always been given exactly the some treatment as the
other prefectures of Japan. For seventy years, since
she came within the administrative fold of the Maiji
Government. Okinawa has continued her existence and

Enclosure to Despatch No. 957 dated April 3, 1947 from the United
States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject "Forwarding
Copy of Petition for Restoration of Islands Belonging to Hokkaide".
COPY
To His Excellency General of the Army
Douglas MecArthur.
P E T I T I O N
We, the undersigned members of the House of Representatives
clected from Hokkaido, have the honor to present to Your Ixcellency
this petition concerning certain islands of the Kurile Archipelago
now under Soviet military occupation, -- to wit:
The Shikotan Group (including Shikotan, Taraku, Shibatou,
Suisho, Yuri, and Akiyuri), constituting a part of Nemuro
Province on the main island of Hokkaido.
The Islands of Southern Kuriles, i.e. Etorofu and Kunashiri.
These islands, which are en integral part of Japan, are now oc-
cupied by the armed forces of the Soviet Union. Since all the com-
munications with Hokkaido heve been cut off, the local populace is
subjected to reat difficulties and hardships. We beg to submit a
statement of our cese as follows:
Statement
1. The Shikotan group which is now under Soyiet Occupation belonged
administratively to the Province of Nemuro on the main island of
Hokkaido even under the regime of the Tokugawa Shogunate. Geograph-
ically, the group lies outside the Kurile Archipelago.
The islands of Etorofu and Kunashiri, situated close to the
Shikotans, were definitely recognized as Japenese territory by the
Russo-Japanese Treaty of Commerce of 1854. The Sagahlien-Kuriles
Exchange Treaty concluded between Japan and Russia in 1875, does not
mention these two islands, but provides only for the exchange of
Uruppu and the islands to the north for Sagahlien.
It will give indisputably a proof thet these two islands as well
as the Shikotan islands were always under Japanese possessions. Certain-
ly not a territory taken from Russia by "violence or greed" to borrow
the words of the Cairo Declaration. It only so happened that follow-
ing the above-mentioned exchange, the Japanese have used the name
"Chishima" as a generic term to cover the whole Kurile Archipelago.
2. Btorofu and Kunashiri as well as Shikotans have been for the past
200 years the home of Japanese fishermen. Their inhabitants are close-
ly bound with the people of Hokkaido economically as well as in blood.
Now the complete severance of communication between these islands
and the main island of Hokkaido have given rise to the following
sisuation:
(a) The fishermen in northern Hokkaido have lost their most
important fishing grounds for crabs, salmon, and ced, and also
rich Kombu (odible ses weed) fielde. The loss of these asee
epesatáng ia d jacent watere. Por, if shey sheald stzey

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Enclosure to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 957,
April 3, 1947.
capture and detention.
(b) In case these islands are placed permanently under Soviet
rule, the proximity of the Japanese and Soviet possessions is
likely to lead to endless troubles and the Japanese fishermen
will be subjected to constant fear.
(c) The 17,000 Japanese settlers on these islands will be
deprived of their livelihood as well as their homesteads. The
settlers if forcibly repatriated to Hokkaido may cause a new
burden to the island that is already suffering from the short-
age of foodstuff.
(a) One-third of Japan's fishery products used to come from
Hokkaido; and one-third of the Hokkaido fishery products from
the Nemuro and Chishime erea. The loss of these islands will
cause acute shortage in the protein food supply for the
Japanese people.
Under the sympathetic guidance of the U.S. Occupation Army the
people of Hokkaido. have made a rapid progress toward democracy. We,
who heve been elected to the Diet es their representatives according
to their freely expressed will, cannot but have deep concern over the
possible plight of that part of our homelend and that section of our
people, who are shut off behind an "iron curtain."
It is reported that the Soviet government has decided to make all
the Soviet occupied territories permanent Soviet possessions. Such a
decision, unilateral as it may well be, cannot but fill us with grave
apprehensions. The undersigned on behalf of our fellow citizens of
Hokkaido do hereby most respectfully appeal to Your Excelloncy for
your good offices in order that these little islands which have been
always Japanese may remain Japanese, and free intercourse may be re-
established between these islands and the main island of Hokkaido.
24th March 1947
Members of the House of
Representatives from Hokiaido.

Japan's southwestern islands, which form & chaind stretching 570
long between Kyushu and Formosa and along the eastern fringe of the a
China Sea, are called collectively the Nansei Islands (Note ).
The archipelago comprises two principal island groups: the Sat
constitutes the main part of Okinawa Prefecture. These groupe an
divided into five sub-groupe, as shown in the table below, there being
islands in all, of which 25 have areas pf 4 squste miles or more (Not a).

There are two other isolated groups, Daito and Sento, which are admm
stratively included also in Okinawa Prefecture. Of these, the Daito group
will be dealt with in another chapter, while the Sento Islands (total area 2
sq. m.) lying north of Sakishima sub-group are uminhabited and of little im-
portance.
The "Nansei" is a geographic term of recent date. On the other hand,.
the southern portion of the archipelago, with Okinawa as the center, which
constituted formerly a semi-independent country, has been known as Ryukyu
(also spelled Riu-Kiu), or more widely among Occidentals, as Luchu.(pened
variously, Lew Chew, Loochoo, Liu Chiur, :etc.). Sometimes, "Ryukyu" is
applied as a generic name to the entire archipelago (Pirective of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, 29 January 1946). Here, however, the.
term is used in the original restricted sense to designate what was formerly
the so called "Kingdom" of Ryukyu (Note 3).
Most of the islands are ragged and barren. A few in the south, having
comparatively large areas of level ground, are mostly composed of elevated
coral reefs. Water supply is generally ina'dequate. Fierce typhoons are
frequent in summer and autumn, while in winter there blows a strong mon-
soon.
The Kuroshio (Japan Stream), which flows northvard along the eastern
coast of Formosa to the west 'of the Nansei Islands, cuts across the island chain
between the Osumí and Amami sub-groups passing out again to the eastern
side.

This can be confirmed by the archeological
the ancesters of the present Ryukyuans kad common
Japanese.
For instance:
1. Small domesticated dogs of the stone age unearthed in the matn
island of Okinawa closely resemble the dogs of the stone age of southern
Kyushu and! Honshu of Japan (Conis familiaris Nipponensis primus). Thoe
dogs are believed to have migrated to these islands together with their masters
(Note 8, Illustration 1).
2. Bones of ancient man unearthed in the main island of Okinawa also
resemble those of the stone age of Japan (Note 9, Illustration II).
3. Old stone implements and earthenwares, in spite of their consider-
able local peculiarities, also reveal features in common with those of Japan
proper, especially southern Kyushu (Note 10).
4. Linguistically, both the Ryukyu language and the Japanse langge
belong to the same system. Sho Sho-ken, a native scholar and statesman of
Ryukyu in the 1 7th century, noted such linguistic connections and propounded
the identity of the origin of the Japanese and Ryukyuans (Note 11).
Commodore Perry who visited Ryukyu in May, 1853 says in his Nerrattve
of the Expedition of an American Squadron to the China Seas and Japan (Chap.
XVII, p. 365) as follows:
 The island seems to be peopled by two distinct races, the Japanese
and the Lew Chewan, properly so called. They both have originally
sprung, however, from the same stock..
The Japanese and the Lew Chewans differ slightly from each other,
the latter being more effeminate and somewhat less intelligent, but this
imay be owing to their simple, retired life, upon a remote island, whrre
their wants are few, and nature is generous. They have, however, ch
strong resemblances that it is almost impossible to resist the conviction
of their sameness of origin.

III. History of the Ryukyu Gremp.
1. The Ryukyu Dynasties.
about the close of the 12th century by Shunten, said to be a
for three generations. In the middle of the 13th century, th
Omanososhbi, a Ryukya oplc of varty satle esatory.

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.98)
the succesding dynasty of Eiso grew in power ao that
such westera islands as Kume, Kerama and Ibeya: It was also im
that even Amami-Oshima was.put under the control of Ryukyu
After the beginning of the 14th century, however, the island was thnown
into chaos, and was divided into three so-called "Hills", each Hill having its
own leader.
About that time, Satto, the leader of the central Hill, paid tribute to China
for the first time at the latter's demand. Eventually the house of Sattq. gained
ascendence over the two other Hills and extended its sway as far south a the
islands of Miyako and Yaeyama.
At the beginning of the 15th century, Sho-Hashi establishing his control
over the southern and northern Hills achieved the unification of the kimgdom
(1429). Prosperity was brought to the country through trade with Japan,
China and south sea regions. This dynasty, after having lasted for six genera-
tions, was replaced by the Sho-En dynasty which continued under 19:rulers
for 410 years up to the days of Meiji. The power of the Ryukyu momarchy
was at its zenith in the days of Sho-Shin, the third ruler of the dynasty, his
influence being extended as far as Amami-Oshima in the north and Sakishima
in the south. In 1609 Ryukyu was conquered by SHiMAZl&, Jehisa, feudal
Lord of Satsuma, and the country wa's definitely placed under Japane suze-
rainty.
2. Relations with Japan.
Acoording to the Nihon Shoki (Chronicle of Japan) written in 720 D.
(the reign of Empress Gensho) and the Shoku Nihongi (Supplemeatary
Chronicle of Japan) written in 797 .D. (the reign of Emperor Kammh).
Japan's relations with Nansei Islands date back as early as 616d (the reiga
of Empress Suiko). It is recorded that tribute bearers from Kume and labi.
gaki islands arrived in Japan in the year, 714 A.D. (the reign of Empress Gan
myo). The people coming from these islands werd cordially received, granted
court ranks and family names. . Some of them settled down in Japan and
were naturalized. The imperial, court also despatched missions to thele far.
oft islands.
Towards the end of the 9th century these relations gradually began to
weaken. Especially from the middle of the 14th century, owing to political
disturbances prevailing in Japan proper as well as in Ryukyu, the offieial inter-
paurse between Japan and Ryukyu was greatly curtailed. It seems, however
that oultural and commercial relations continued to be maintained even during
that period. Ancient tiles considered to be those of the Heian and Kamakura
eras of Japan (the 11th—12th centuries) have been unearthed in Ryukyu.
and many other facts have come to light, showing the influence of pld Japanese
culture upon religion, architecture and painting. The offcial documents in

Ryukyu in those days were written in Japanese (Illustration ll)- tadad

3. Relations with China.
It is generally believed that China's relatons wtih Ryukyu began ia 1372
when Tai-Tzu, founder of the Ming dynasty dispatched a mission to demand
the Ryukyuans topay tribute to his court (Note 18). The clan of the Central
Hill complied and secured China's assistance, unified the country and set up
the Ryukyu dynasty. Thereafter missions bearing gifts were regularly sent
from Ryukyu to China.
China sent a mission whenever a new suler was installed in Ryukyu to
grant him recognition. No Chinese functionaries were stationed in Ryukyu
(Note 19).
B.H. Chamberlain says in his essay "The Lüchu Islands and their in.
habitants" published in The Geographical Journal, Vol. V, April, 1895, p. 344.:
The Chinese over-lordship, on the other hand, was rather nominal
than real, and the so-called tribute-ships despatched annually to Puchan
did such good strokes of business under the rose, that the Luchuans
actually requested to be allowed to send more tribute to China than the
amount originally stipulated!
Further, in the same page he writes "tribute ships—in other words,
trading-ships".

IV. History of the Sateumam Group.
The northern half of the Nansei Islands which constitutes a part of Kago-
shima Prefecture, was historically bound with closer ties with Japan peeger
than the Ryukyu group. The fact that the inhabitants of these islands together.
with Ryukyuans maintained contact with the Imperial Court of Yamtato 
the 7th century is recorded in early Japanese chronicies. (see page 6). 
the Sth century Japan established the Province of Tane, comprielng Tamega
shima and adjacent islands, which was placed under a governer 
by the central government. When Tane Province was abolfshed l , the
island group was incorporated into the Prevince of Osumi, Kyushu, a 
tinued to remain a Japanese possession, being used sometimes a a pl
colony.
In 1185 when the Tairas, which had dominated Japan, were drieem t
of power by the rival clan of Minamoto, a number of the defeste claasme
fled to Kyushu—thence to these islands as far south as Tokara and Ammah
where they settled permanently. Today'there are on these stands memy
families who trace their ancestry to the ancient clan of Taira (Nete 2o).
In 1188, SHiMAZU, Tadahtma, the first feudal lord of Setsume'm seu-
 Kyusku, was appointed overlord of the "Pwelye Lalands on  S
Sea", which are preswmably, to be identifßed with the tetends of the

government began to wane from the close of the 9th century, it was spat up
among powerful families, who are believed to have moved in from Ryukyu,
and who plunged the islands into the turmoil of petty warfate. In f266 the.

This sub-group was the first to be occupied by the conquering atimy f

Shimazu clan, whilt Ryukyu itself was made a sort of protectorate (see page

The Shimazus stationed magistrates and numerous administrative oietals
in the islands, and encouraged the sugar industry which came to be am im-
portant source of the clan revenue.
Like the Tokaras, the Amami Islands also provided refuge for the Taira
clansmen, whose settlement on the islands together with the later infux of
Shimazu officials undoubtedly served to infuse the blood of the istanders with
a fresh Japanese strain. This group was with the rest of the Shimazu dpmain
incorporated into Kagoshima Prefecture after the Meiji Restoratio.

In 1879, when General U.S. Grant, the retired Prendent of U.S.A.,
visited Japan, he exercised good offices between the two governments i their
controversy over the Ryukyu question and advised China to seek an ami-
cable settlement through direct negotiation with Japan (Note 26).
The negotiation was thus resumed between the Japanese Minister Shishi-
do, and the Chinese Tsungli-Yamen. On October 21, 1880, poth parties
agreed upon the draft of treaties to the effect that Japan would cede to China
a part of her territory, namely the two southernmost island groups of Ryukyu:
Miyako and Yaeyama (Note 27), and that China would permit Japanese sub-
jects to carry on commerce in the interior of China. It was also agreed that
the ceremony of signing the treaties would take place within tem days and the
exchange of ratifications in about three months. However, the Chinese Gov-
ernment deferred signing, and replied finally on November 17 that the treaties
could got be signed as prior authorisation of the Southern and Northern Com-
mercial Ministers had not been secured. Thus the negotiation was dropped,
and Japan continued to hold as before all the Ryukyu Islands..
It was British Minister Sir Thomas Wade who used his good oices in this
incident.

Religion
Buddhism and Shintoism are ingrained in the daily life:of the p
Okinawa Prefecture. The first Shinto shfine was astablished about the
of the 15th century, while Buddhist temples were introduced by
priests in still earlier days. Before the war there wese 8,:shrimes and 32
temples.
There still exists a very popular aboriginal religion. It bear
likeness to the originat Shintoism of Japan.
There-are also influences of Taoism and Confucianism. Some Dokwa
(Taoist temple) and Bunbyo (Confucian shrine) are traceable directly to
Chinese origin, but most of the customs in Ryukyu which retain the in@wence
of these cult have been transplanted through Japan.
Christianity was first introduced when a European ship came to'the
Yaeyama Island Group in the beginning of the 17th century: Around he.
middle of 19th century Dr. Bernard Jean Bettelheim, an Engltsh misstonary,
worked zealously to spread the Gospel. However, the free propafation of
Christianity became possible after the Meiji Era when the anti-christian policy
of the Tokugawa Shogunate was discarded and the freedom of religion was
established. Before the war there were r8 churches and missions, repre
senting various denominatións.

6. Meteorological Observatories.
There are several important meteorological observatories—namely,
Amami-Oshima, the main island of Okinawa, Ishigaki and at other plage
The Nansei Islands lie in the path of the typhoon which frequently v
Japan from summer to autumn, and which generally changes its course when
it reaches the islands, namely around 27° N. The typhoon forcast ls mot
possible in Japan without meteorological observation in the Nansel Is
In winter too, the observation of the cyclons moving over these islands Iis
needed for forecasting the atmospheric changes in Japan.

4. Industries.
The agricultural production of the islands is insignifcant, owtng'to
in a very primitive way on small farms which dot steep hilieldes.
Less than 2 per cent of the arable, land is planted to upland rics
tants used to look to the matnland for almost all of their staple foon
supply.
Taken as a whole, the islands' production is not considerable; and
they have imported always from the mainland more than they mpory
thereto. (In 1936, total of imports 1,456,496 yen, that of exports 1,018.
405 yen).
5. Other Facts.

more than a minor coastal enterprise. However, the tslands serve es
convenient base for tuna and bonito fishing vessels of the 
operating in 'the western Pacisc. At the same time, no m

Chapter II. The Velonine (Ktmen) e
Greegp
1. Physical Geography.
A Group of 8 islands and several rocks lying at a polns abode
miles to the south-west bf the Bonin Islands are generally kmowm 
Volcano (Kazan) Islands or Iwo (Sulphur) Islands. The biggedt telana
is Iwo-jima (7.79 sq. m.). These islands though sometimes included tm
the Bonins, are of a later origin geographically having sprung tato 
istence in the Quaternary Period; their volcanic activities stim contma
2. History.
The existence of the Volcano Islands was not conarmed until 177
when Captain Cook's Third Expedition saw three islands at a distance
Island". Previoualy, in 1548, it is said, Bernardo de Torres, Spantah
explorer, sighted alsó three lslands between 24º and 25° N., with a volcano
on one of them, and named them "Los Volcanos". . This latitudinal mee-
surement, however, differs from that given by the Cook Expedition. Fus
quite a long time thereafter, not a few maps continued to treat the "Los
Volcanos" of Torres and the "Sulphur Island" of the Cook Expedition
as different islands. The fact is that these islands had been seen merely
from a distance, and no particular country had laid claim to their pos
.session. They remained uninhabited until the end of the 1oth century.
TAKASAKI, Goroku, Governor of TOKYO Prefecture, who, during
his inspection trip in Bonins, visited the Volcano Islands in November
1887, is presumed to be the frst man to land there. In September, 1801,
the islands were formally declared to be Japanese possessions and placed
under the jurisdiction of Tokyo Prefecture. It is about his times.that
Japanese settlers began to arrive.
3. Inhabitants.
The population as of June 1st, 1944 was 1,029, most of them Hving
on Iwo-jima, the largest of the three islands. A few also live on North
Iwo-jima, bnt there is no settler on South Iwo-jima.
All the inhabitants here are either those who have immigrated trom
the mainland or their descendants. They are engaged in the cultivatiom
-of sugar-cane and of coca. No naturalized foreignèrs or their desoen
dants live here as they do in the Bonins.

TOP SECRET

PRESIDENT TRUMAN TO GENERALISSIMO STALIN
AUGOST 27, 1945 *
(Files of the Executive Secretariat)
In response to your message of August 22nd, 1945, as far as
the base on the Kurile Islands is concerned, my idea was that use
of landing rights in the central Kuriles during the occupation of
Japan would be an important contribution to the cooperative action
we will be taking in connection with the carrying out of the Japanese
surrender terms as it would afford another route for air connection
with the United States for emergency use during the period of
occupation of Japan.
/8-2745
I also felt no hesitancy in bringing up the matter of landing
facilities for commercial use. You evidently misunderstood
my message because you refer to it as a demand usually laid
before a conquered state or an allied state unable to defend parts
of its territory. I was not speaking about any territory of the Soviet
Republic. I was speaking of the Kurile Islands, Japanese territory,
disposition of which must be made at a Peace settlement. I was
advised that my predecessor agreed to support in the peace settlement
the Soviet acquisition of those Islands. I did not consider it offensive
when you asked me to confirm that agreement. When you expect
our support for your desire for permanent possession of all the
Kurile Islands, I cannot see why you consider it offensive if I ask
for consideration of a request for landing rights on only one of
those Islands. I consider the request for discussion all the more
reasonable because of the close and cordial relations existing
between our two governments and between us personally. While I
believe early discussion of these matters would be helpful, I will
not press it if you do not wish to discuss them now.
TOP SEORET FILE
SEP 1 3 1956
*Drafted in the Department
TOP SECRET

Enclosure to C.L.O.No
PETITION FOR THE RETURN OF HADOMAI BOLAN
AND THE ISLANDS OF ETOROFU AND KUNASHIRI
Your Excellency General of the Army
Douglas Macarthur,
13 August 1947
The Habomai Islands (including Shikotan, Taraku,
Shibatsu, Suisho, Yuri and Akiyuri) and Etorofu and
Kunashiri Islands of Kuriles now under the occupation
of Soviet Army are an integral part of Japan and also
the place where we can get great amount of fishery
products as the sourge of protein food supply absolutely
necessary for the sustainance of Japanese people.
Therefore we take this opportunity to lay a. petition before
Your Exceliency concerning the return of these islands.
Staterent
1. The Habomai Island (including Shikotan, Taraku,
Shibatsu, Suisno, Yuri and Akiyuri) which are now under
the occupation of soviet Forces have belonged administre-
ti ely to the Province of Nemuro of Hokkaido Mcin Land
since under th regime of the !a: distant Tokugawa Sho-
gunate and ne been includer in the Kurile Islands.
While, of the cent ui Islands, Etorofu and Kuna-
shiri were definitely recognized as the Japanese territory
by the Russo-Japanese Treaty of Commerce of 1854. Another
evidence to prove this is the fact that the Saghlien-Kuriles
Exchange Treaty concluded between Japan and mussia 1n 1875
does not mention these two islands, but provides only for the
exchange of Uruppu and other 17 islands to the north thereof
for Saghalien, thus excluding these two islands from the
object of exchange.
Therefore the Habomai Islands as well as Etorofu and
Kunashiri Islands were always under Japanese possession
and not a territory transferred from Bussia.
Etorofu and Kunashiri as well as the Habomai Islands have
been for the past 200 years the home of Japanese engaging
in fisheries, with no settlement of any alsien nation. Im
other word Japanese people have been living there for more
than l00 years before the Meiji Restoration, t efr inhabi-
tants have lived there through three to six gonerations eme
closely bound with the people of Hokkaido Lain Land econom-
cally.
2. The Russian Army, however, took over these islands
under military occupation suddenly on 1 eptember 1945 and
cut off all the communications between there and Hokkaido
as if by ah "iron curtain".
At the begining of the occupation some of the inhake-
tants were very much seared and frightened that they mames
to: extrieate their beloved old parents, wives and ehilasem

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
secretly by small boats to the coast of Nemuro, Hokkaide.
Although these island lie just in the hailing distance frem
Nemuro, these refug can not hear anything from their
dependable husbands and sons stranded there, and are tery
much anxious about them day and night. To add to their
grievances, it has been reported of late that all the
areas now under Soviet occupation will be integrated as
the territory of Soviet Russia. Such a report can not make
us foresee a gloomy prospect on this problem.
We have surrendred to the Allied Powers by accepting
the terms set forth by the Potsdam Proclamation and we are
making our best efforts in faithfully carrying out the
proviseions thereof. Now the Proclamation provides that.
"Japanese soverignty shall be limited to the islands of
Honshu, Hokkaido, Kyushu, Shikoku and such minor 1slands
as we determine," and no decision has yet been made of
the disposal of the Habomai Islands, Btorofu and Kunashiri.
These islands, however, do not fall under the category of
"territories which she (Japan) has taken by violanee and
greed" as provided by the Cairo Declaration, but have
been an integral part of Japan historically and practically.
Therefore we are convinced that you will be sure to return
these islands to us in the future.
3. About one third of fishery products of Japen has
hitherto come from Hokkaido, of which one third is produeed
in these waters. As these islands are completely separated
from Hokkaido, we have lost an important area of Sishery
producing salmon, trout, codfish, crab and seeweed, and are
suffering from the shortage of protein food supply caused
thereby. In addition t: this, fishing in the adjacent
waters in misty weather, fishermeh are very apt: unwittingly
to take a wrong course and to trespass on the Soviet's waters,
resulting in their arrest or detention by Soviet Authorities.
Therefore the fishermen around these waters are very much
afraid to go out fishing.
In case these islands become the territory of Soviet
Russia, the proximity of territories of two countries will
becontinous cause of fishery trouble, precipitate  the
Japanese fishermen into a great difficulty under fear and
oppression and also we shall be deprived of the birthplaces
of about 17,000 inhabitats imbued with the efforts and
sufferings of their ancestors.

HEADQUARTERS RYUKYUS COMMAND
WTC/rba
OFFICE OF THE CHEMICAL OFFICER
APO 331
7 October 1947
CURRENT REPORT OF CHEMICAL SERVICE (VIP)
I. COMMAND
1. The Chemical Service, RYKON, is commanded by lst Lt. William T.
Carney, CML C, who assumed command on 16 August 1947.
II. NISSION
1. To advise and assist the Commanding General, RYkOl,in matters
pertaining to Chemical Corps supply and training.
2. Operation of the RYkO Chemical Corps Depot.
III. SUBORDINATE UNITS
1. RYKOM CHEMICAL CORPS DEPOT
2. 9OTH CHEMICAL SERVICE DETACHMENT (PS)
IV. FUNCTIONS
1. The 9Oth Chemical Service Detachment (PS), having a strength
of one (1) officer and seventeen (17) enlisted men is the only Chemi-
cal Corps unit in the Ryukyus Command at this time. This detachment,
plus two (2) War Department Civilian Employees, operates the RYKOM
Chemical Corps Depot.
2. All Chemical Corps supplies necessary to the requirements of
this Command are received, stored and issued by the RYKOk Chemical
Corps Depot.
V. PRESENT ACTIVITIES
1. Receipt storage and issuè of Class II, IV and V Chemical Corps
supplies for the Ryukyus Command.
2. On-job-training of enlisted personnel.
3. Surveillance, segregation and rehabilitation of supplies and
equipment that have become unserviceable.
-1-

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.98)
CURRENT REPORT OF CHEZICAL SERVICE (VIP)(Cont'd)
4. Shipment of excess items to other commands which are in short
supply.
5. Shipment of excess critical items to Z/I.
6. Delivery of all surplus to Chinese government has been com-
pleted.
VI. FUTURE PLAIS
1. Continuation of present activities.
2. One small surplus declaration is contemplated in the near
future to dispose of supplies that are found to be unservicoable and
uneconomically repairable when turned in by using units.
3. Formulation of plans to take over Chemical Corps supply for
Air Force and Navy if called upon to do so.
WILLIAM T CARNEY
lst Lt.,
CML C
Chemical Officer
-2-

OPERATION ICDERO" AND THE ASL OF
s/agt Howard Goodhow
Al thoush Olinawe was a word added to the vocabulary of the mes o the
street only after the Easter invasion of that island, the actual military
operations against the enemy there were begun over six months befere Amerleem
troops stormed ashore. It was on 10 October 1g44 that carrier based planes
staged a severe bombing raid on the island, completely demolishing the ege-
old city of Neha and other vital military installations, and causing the
Jepaneso army; which had only arrived in force in June of that year, to start
the elaborate cave fortifications which proved so offective in the campai
to follow.
However, more important than the damage inflicted were the reconnals-
sance photographs made at the same time which provided the essential details
for preinvasion planning by the Tenth Army.
TENTH ARMY ACTIVATED AT FORT 'SAM!
Originally activated at Tort Sam Houston. Texas, the Tenth Army was com
manded by Lt General Simon Bolivar Buckmer, re-conqueror of the Aleutians.
From "Tort. Sam" it moved to Oahu, T. H., where it began the planning of oper
ation "Causeway", the invasion of Tormosa. However, it was decided to by
pass this island in favor of Okinawa.. Oahu was an ideel spot for the pre
paration of the new attack, since immense supplies wero on hand and many per-
sonnel skilled in planning invasions wero already stationed there.
In addition, many formor Okinewans were living in Hawail as naturalised
citizens of the United States. Okinawa, on the other hand, was the only is
land large amough to support the 26 projected airfiolds for the bombing of
Japan proper with land-based planos, and at the same time provide room for a
taging area for the vast number of troops and supplios required to sepport
the ultimato invasion of Japan.
OPERATION 'ICEBERG' CONCEIVED
With the information and experience gained in planning the inyasion of
Formosa. the Tenth Army wns ready. for oporation "Iceberg" the assault upon
Okinawa.
Many plans for new equipment, such as recoilless cannon, "leaflet tanks",
M-26 General Perahing tanks and the new M-15 mobile 155 mm gun carriages had
to be shelved in favor of the top priority of the European Theater of Oper

the than"top secret" vT radio operated fuze which hnd aided in winning the

In the first part of March, the advance parties from Teath Aray assemtaea

TOKYO ROSE GUESSES
Japan hazarded her guess at the probable destination of the troop laden
convoys which the enemy now knew were unaerwey. Through the Tokyo Rose pro-
gram she "informed" the Tenth Army men that they were sailing to their "death"
to invade the first home island of the Japanese Impire - Okinawa. On 17 March
1945, the initial pre-invasion tactics began with repeated air strikes on
Okinawa by the famed Task Force 58, which only shortly beforo had been causing
the Japanese homeland no end of trouble. The men laughed as their own ships
were "sunk" and "re-sunk" over the Japanese propaganda programs, but began
to consider seriously tho job phead as some of our naval losses were confirmed
and the call to battle stations was sounded more frequently in the convoy.
SURFAC BOMBARDMENTS BEG IN
Thon on 25 March 1945 the initial surface bombardmente of Okitawa were
begun by Task Torce 54. ▲ day later landings were made on Kerame Retto by
units of the 77th Infantry Division. That group of islands southwest of
Okinawa was secured by 29 March. providing a safe passage for the major in-
vasion fleet which passed in the channel between them and the larger island.
At the same time, daring units of the Navy began underwater reconnais
sance of the two invasion points - the southeastern Okinawa beaches and the
Hagushi shoreline near Kadena and Yontan airfields, which had already been
put out of commission by the preliminary bombardments. The Navy units came
to the shores and removed underwater obstacles and mines which had been
cleverly planted to hamper the invasion of the coral reefed island - a base
large enough to accommodate the entire Japanese Navy in its pre-war maneuvers.
FEINT ATTACK MADE IN SOUTHEAST
Finally, on Easter Morning, 1 April 1945, the Japanese observed the firet
landing attempts on Okinawa as the 2nd Marine Division, with attached Army
troops, began a feint attack on the southeast coast to draw the enemy's at-
tentton from the main landing which began at 0830 on the Hagushi beaches..
The 7th and 96th Army Divisions and the 1st and 6th Marine Divisions
found comparatively slight resistance to their landings at Hagushi. Unper-
turbed Okinawa farmers continued to plow their fields as these strangers
streamed ashore.
In one day the beachhoad was established with the capture of both
Kadena and Yontan airfields. By 2 April the 7th Army Division had completely
severed the island in a drive to the west coast. By 5 April Phase I of the
campaign was completed. The XIv Army Corps. under command of May General
John R Hodge (now Lt Gen), formed a battle line to the south. while the III
Amphibious Marine Corps under Lt General Gaiger began its push to the north-
ern tip of the island.
PREPARED DEFENSES RLACHED IN SOUTH
On the southern army front scattered Japanese resistance gave may until
army units reeched the first prepered defense area on the approaches to the
Machinato line, running from the north end of Machinato airstrip to the north
end of Yonabaru airstrip. and including Kakasu and Skyview (Hacksaw) ridges.
Here, on 7 April, the 96th "Deadeye" Division engaged in the fiercest artil-
lery duel of Pecific warfare with the Japanese 32nd Army.

96TH, 27TH HELD UP AT MACHINATO
On the south front the 96th and 27th Divieions' advance was still held up

firing out of the solid coral hills.. Disabled tanks were completely demolished
by Jap "satchel charges" dropped inside the turrets at night.
On 24 April a 27th Division combat team finally broke through the cave
fortifications of Kakaru ridge and "Item pocket," on the Jap left flank, and
encircled Skyfiew ridge. Then the 77th Division from Ie Shima came into line,
replacing the 96th which had suffered more than 50 per cent casualties in the
battle.
The enemy troops still in the Hacksaw ridges fortifications were finally
eradicated with burning gasoline, flame throwers and a final asseult by the 77th
Division from the crest of the hill with cargo nets and hand grenades in some
of the fiercest hand-to-hand battles of the campaign.
JAPS TINALLY RETREAT
Finally, the main element of the Japanese force retreated, only slightly
further south, to the most formidable defenses of the campaign, the Mahe-Shuri
Yonabaru line, lanked by Conical hill on the Jap right and Sugar Loaf hill on
the left. The Japs counter-attacked fiercely at night with landings above
Yonabaru and at Machinato in small ten-man boats, and with infiltrations of
hundreds of troops through the very center of our lines.
The infiltrations resulted in the enemy reocoupying a strong point alpost
two miles behind the line for over three days. Other units of the Jap spesial

MARINES TAKE SUGAR LOAF, NAHA

Meantime, the rainy season on Okinawa had set in, and like the mud, the
Japanese army oozed southward, never establishing a main line of defense. We
pushed rapidly onward and the 7th Division reached the southeast coast of the

troops in the southern outskirts of Naha Town.
JAPS RETREAT TO LAST LINE

their push until finally the Jap line held at their last strongpoint, YaefuDake
escarpment, running east from Itoman. Increasing mortar supporting fire was
needed and our dangerously low supplies had to be augmonted by air freight,
since sbveral vital ammunition ships had fallen prey to the deadly Baka bomb
piloted by the suicidal Jap Kamikaze corps.
Army and navy guns shelled the last stronghold and Marine planes dropped
makeshift "napalm" bombs, made with auxiliary airplane gas tanke filled with
flame-thrower fluid and ignited with an impact fuse. Five handred foot hoses
were attached to flame-throwing tanks and the cliff defenses were saturated
with gasoline which was touched •ff with dynamite charges to clean the Japs
from this last stronghold.
GENERAL BUCKNER KILLED
On 18 June 1945. the Tenth Army was shocked by the death of its commander.
General Buckmer, who was killed as he viewed the battle from an observation
point on the front lines. Had he Tived but three days longer, he mould have

Rumors of a purported large-scale Japanese re-invasion of Okineme 
Commander-in-Chief of Okinawa troops, didn't believe it. He and his ehief of
staff committed hari-kari, leaving behind his personal cook to'relate the
lurid story of the traditional ceremony and to reveal the Babulous Jap head

GENERAL STILLWELL TAKES COMMAND
On 23 June, General Stillwell took command of the Tenth Army, vacating
his position as Chief of Army Ground Torces. Several other phases of the
campaign were cancelled, and other malle? islands, such as Iheya Shime.
Kume Shima and Aguni Shima were taken without event by reconnaissance units.
As a security measure a final meticulous sweep of the island was mede, this
time from south to north. Caves were' sealed with bulldosers and blasting
charges, and isolated snipers eliminated.
slight opposition was encountered in the area south of Kadena, but many
isolated groups of Jap fanatics held out for months in northern Okinawa.
RYUKYUS SURRENDER COMPLETED
On 10 September 1945, surrender of the Ryukyus was completed on the same
spot at Tenth Army Headquarters on which the flag was first raised upon seour-
ing the island. The abandonment of the other phases proved a wise move, since
the ememy did not even have a single plane or ship with which to bring its
generals to the surrender ceromonies from such cave-entrenched major islands
as Miyako, Okino and Amami; their airfields were in such a shambles that no
planes could use them.
The final ceremony was simple and informal and typical of the way General
Stillwell got things done. To start it off with a bang and a touch of humor.
the band struck up "The Old Grey Mare", following with the GIs' favorite,
"Tho Beer Barrel Polka." After about 20 minutes of music the Japanese
military representatives walked out to take their places, standing in back of
the surrender table.
On their left was a platoon honor guard of precision marines: at their
left rear a platoon of kheki clad Army infantrymen; in front of them a double
row of U.s. Army and Navy genaral officers. The area was surrounded with 25
spanking new General Pershing tanks, and 15 or more 155mm mobile gun carriages.
all with their open barrels pointed down the Japs throats.
The witnesses to the ceremony were a crowd of camere-clicking GIs and
officers who perched themselves on every vantage point. Promptly at 1430
the General's flourishes were sounded, and General Stillwell, followed closely
by his Chief os Staff, General Merrill. strode out of his camouflaged head-
quarters quonset to take his place at attention in front of the table. It was
one of the few occasions on which the well liked general wore the four silver
stars of his rank, but he wore no other decorations. A few curt orders through
an interpreter, the Japs signed, then General Stillwell signed.
AERIAL PARADE ENDS CEREMONY
An aerial parade roared overhead, starting with the majestic B-29s at
high altitude and followed by other bombers anf fighters until the cooky little
Mustangs skimmed the flagpole. General Stillwell was heard to remark, as the
defeated Jape marched off "There go the warlords of the Pacific". as the
realisation of the complete victory brought jubiliation for the first time.
GIs, Generals and admirals mingled, shook hands and slapped backs.

HI STORY OF OKINAWA
Okinawa is not merely tho island of the swoet
potato, the habu, the Gook, and the "forgotton G.I."
It is — or was — a glittoring Oriental state in
microcosm. Its history has all the trappings of its
more protentious neighbors -- logodary herpes, tho
riso and fall of dynastios, alternating poriods of
vigorous indepondence and foroign domination, groat
artists and lordly patrons of thc arts, sagos, saints
and statesmcn, virtuous and voluptuous womon, philo-
sophers and philandorors, pious priosts and pationt
poasants, overy elomont, in fact, of a varicd and
often exciting society. Whatover it may bo or soom
to be today, Okinawa has not been historically a for-
sakon Pacific isle ronoto from oven thc backwash of
civilization.
Okinawan history bcgins as it rocently soamod to
cnd — in chaos. In the boginning. according to tho
Omoro, Okinawa's Book of Gonosis, was Todo-ko, tho
Sun god, and chaos. Through Todo-ko's intervontion,
chaos was resolved into land and sea. Tho first
land to materialize was the mimute island of Kutaka
Shima, several miles off southeastern Okinawa.
There two dem-Gods. Shinireku and Mamiko, performed
momumental labors. They carried stone and sand and
earth, hewed rock, and planted trees and grasses.
They arrested the floods which regularly imundated the
island. Later they wrested fire from the jealous fire
breathing Sea Dragon. Finally. through the mediation
of the Wind, they produced offspring - mortal men,
who anjoyed the fruits of the land and the blessings
of fire. Presently the descendants of these men
amigrated to Okinawa, a vast new universe to which
their own labors gave final shape.
Written records of tho Ryukyus begin about 600
AD in Chinese and Japanese documents. In 605 and 606
the Ohinese engaged in an a-typical episode of foreign
expansionism when they sont missions to Okinawa to
demand tribute and submission, demands which the
Okinawans refused with vigor and impunity. The Jap-

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.97)
anese in 714 indulged in a correspondingly a-typical
indifference toward the Ryukyus, imoring an open
invitation'from the Northern Ryukyu islanders to
investieato the group. During the next few centuries
contacts with China and Japan were infrequent and
irregular. The period was of independent Okinawan
dovelopment with the island divided into many tiny
feudal states whoso potty lords built themselvos
fortified castles and supported small armies of re-
tainers.

LANGUAGE:
Standard Japanese has been taught in Ryukyus
schools for more than 50 years and is widely spoken
throughout the islands. Even before modern education
came along, Japanese as spoken in Okinama's court
circiles.
The Okinarans, hovever, do have colloquialisms
which are not readily intelligible to the Jananese.
Some of these are hangovers from their aboriginal
language, commonly called Luchuan. This language
no longer is commonly spoken, oxcept perhaps in the
most isolated areas of the Ryukyus. Pronounciations
vary somevhat throughout the islands, just as they
do from one area to another back in the United States.
Very fen Ryukyuans are proficient in English
sinco it vas not stresscd in thoir schools nearly as
much as it has been in Japan. Once in a while one
finds a native who has returned from Hamaii or South
America, bringing back a knonledge of English, Spanish
or Portuguose, but thesc persons are few in number.
POPULATION:
The Ryukyus Islands have been heavily populated
for centuries. When Commodore Perry visited in 1853
he estimated that the Great Loo Choo (Okinava) had
from 150,000 to 200,000 inhabitants, or 310 to 412
persons por square mile.
The first official census made by the Japanese
Government in 1920 indicated a total population through-
out the Ryukyus of 856,&92. Emigration from the
islands has beon quite common, however, and by 1940
the population had dropred to 839,449. Of this number,
435,681 persons lived on Okinawa.
Virtually all tho inhabitants of the Ryukyus,
for a census purpose may be classed as Japanese.
A census report for 1937 showed only 66 foreigners
living on Okinava. Theso were: listed 25 Chinese,
20 Hamaiians, 13 Americans, 6 Russians, 1 Filipino
and 1 Canadian.

Probably the most conspicuous man-made foature of

Tho oarliest practico of the Okinawan people was

symbolism of the tomb was thereforo that of long life.

GOVERINMENT:
Prior to tho 15th contury.Okinawa had bocomo

with tho king surroundod by culturod noblos and supportod

Undor tho now Japanoso rulo tho Rru'yu Islands

islands from Yoron Jina north and for tho southorn
part of Kyushu. Normally tho profocturo has boon
tho connocting link botwoon tho Imporial Govornment
and tho local govornment.
The Goyornor. A govornor, appointed by tho
Primo Ministor upon tho rocommondation of tho Ministry
of Homo Affairs, has boon in chargo of each profocturo.
Tho govornors actod both as represontativos of tho
Imporial Govornmont and as tho honds of the local
self-govornmont. In 1940 the govornor of Ckinawa
Profecturo was Fusataro Fuchigami, 47 yoars old,
who had studiod political scionco at Takyo Imporial

His anmual salary was  4,650. Tho governor had an

Polico.

prostige and power. Employees of high rank commonly

populated aroas of tho islands, Naha (population 65.

POLIT ICS
5. Tvo political parties have been activated on Okinara. The Okinawan
Peoples Party vhich might be considered the labor party and the Okinawan
Democracy Union vhich could be considered the conservative party.
The objectives of the Okinavan Democracy Union aro to secure
democratization in politics, economics, society, culture and education on
Okinawa. The Union shall carry on all activities necessary to accomplish
these objectives.
The principles of the Okinawan People's Party arettorrepresenttthe
interests of all the vorking people such as laborers, farmers, fisherman,
selaried men, small tradesmen and small mamufacturers, and to fight all
feudalistic conservatisms and reactions in accordance with the spirit of the
Potsdam Declaration, and to establish democracy in political, social and
-4-

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.96)
cultural fields during the restoration of Okinawan self-government.
6. No organized political parties exist in the Northern Ryukyus and
there are no indications that any parties are being formed.

LEGAL
Courte
1. The Civilian Courts tried two hundred and ten cases in Okinarar:
Gunto during August. Most of these dealt vith petty larceny, blackmarket-
ing, traffic violations and violations of fish and game laws.
2. The Magistrate Courts, which try misdemeanor cases, commenced
to function in Okinawa Gunto on 25 August 1947.
3. The Supreme Board of Justice in the Northern Ryukyus has promul-
gated and published orders #5 and #6 which provide and set up certain
standards and a definate procedure for qualification, examination and appoir
ment of Civilian Judges, Public Procurators, and Attorneys who would be
permitted to practige in the civilian courts.
The Supreme Board of Justice on 5 August submitted a rcquest for
the expansion of the Civilian Court system in the Northern Ryukyus. It was
recommended that a Court of Appeals composed of a Chief Judge and two
Associate Judges be eetablished who would have jurisdiction over all
appeals from the Civilian District Courts. It was further recommended
that four additional district courts be established to be known as the Koniy
Tokunoshima, Erabu and Kikai District Courts.
4. Of the 22 cases tried by the Civilian Court in the Northern Ryukyus
during August, 12 cases were in violation of the National Mobilisation Law;
two each of larceny, fraud and assault; one each for forgery, trespass,
cruel and inhuman conduct and possession and use of dangerous weapons.
litary Courts

Telephone and Telegraph
28. New telephone exchange offices were opened at Ogimi and Kunigami
on Northern Okinawn. They are tied into the island system by circuits
from Nago.
29. Telephone exchanges on Okinawa operate from 0700 to 1730
seven days a week. A night duty system for typhoon warning was put into
effect during August.
30. A tatal of 10,218 long distance telephone calls were made during
August in Okinawe Gunto. In July 21,396 long distance calls were made in
the Northern Ryukyus.
31. Six officials of the Department of Communications, Okinavan
Civilian Administration went to Naze, Amami O-Shima, to make a survey of
electrical communications to determine what equipment and upplies are
needed.
32. In July 4,702 domestic telegrams were sent in the Northern Ryukyus,
1,921 were dispatched to Japan and 1,949 were received from Japan.
33. The Wire Communication Department of Okinawan Civilien Adminis-
tration employs 77 persons, the telephone exchanges employ 15 and
telephone subscribers number 197.

Chapter I. Minor Islands in the Pacifle
Introduction.
Japan's so-called outlying Pacific Islands include the Dafto (Oagari)
Island Group, Parece Vela (Oki-no-Tori-shima) and Marcus (Minami-
Tori-shima) Islands. In addition to them, Ganges (Naka-no-Tori) also
 sometimes included (Note 1) which however in reality is pon-existent
During the period from May 27th to June 2nd, 1933, the Japanese warshtp
Komahashi explored an expanse 70 miles with the island's supposed posi-
tion as the center but failed to find it. As a result the naval authorlties,
on November 13th, 1933, notified the interested quarters under the jurle-
diction of the Navy Ministry that the island did not exist, although this
information was withheld from the public at that time (Note 2).
I. Daito Island Gróup.
1. Grography.
The Daito Island Group is isolated in mid-ócean, consisting of North
and South Daito Islands (131°14' E. and 25°50' N.) about 195 miles east
of the Main island of Okinawa and of Oki-no-Daito Island (181'11' .
and 24'28' N.) about 80 miles to the south of the two islands. Admini-
stratively, the Island Group belongs to Okinawa Prefecture, but, it is
separated from the Ryukyu Islands not only by a great distance but also
by the Ryukyu Trench 6,000 to 7,000 meters in depth. Furthermore,
unlike Okinawas these Islands are all elevated coral reefs, each with a
circular basin in the center. The whole islands are composed of coral
limestone, but North and South Daite Islands contain each in the center
a seam of claysoll which is fit for the cultivation of the sugar-cane.
Phosphate also is produced on North Daito Island and Oki-no-Dalto
Island. The largest of the three is South Daito Island which has area
of 5.01 square miles.
2. History.
Maps made in Europe early in 17th century indicated an island named

naval officer, Ponafidin, in 1820, after the name of the warship
sighted the islands. "Rase" dates back to 1807, when a French
sighted and so named the island.
For a long time these islands were left ownerless and u
(Note 1). In 1885 the Japanese Government had the Governo
nawa Prefecture inspect North and South Dafto Islands, and ind
them into Japanese territory (Note 5). Thereafter varlous pl
made for opening up of these islands, the development work be
in hand on South Daito Island in 1900 and on North Daito Island
Oki-no-Daito Island was visited in 1892 by the Japanese
Kaimon and formally incorporated into Japanese territory on
17th, 1900.. The work of developing the island was commenced  1
3. Inhabitanls.
The first settlers to arrive in the Daito Islands were 28 inhabe
of Hachijo-jima, Tokyo Prefecture. For this reason, the domimamt
fluence on the islands used to be exercised by persons hailing from th
island.
In subsequent years, natives of Okinawa Prefecture also came
these islands, and they outnumbered the settlers from Hachijo-fima.
stituting over 60 percent of the whole population. Most of them,
ever, having come as casual laborers did not remain permanently ont
islands.
Before the war, the inhabitants of North and South Daito Iak
numbered respectively about 1,800 and 3,500 and they were largely
bers or tenant-farmers of the Dai Nippon Sugar Company.

4. Induntry.
The task of opening up the Daito Islands was undertaken entirely by
Japanese. In the space of some 30 years after the work was started
the beginning of the present century the islands underwent a rapld dev
lopment, in the manufacture of sugar and in the mining of phosphate.
The yearly output of sugar.amounted approximately to between 6.0
and 10,000 metric tons which corresponds to the total volume of Japame
sugar rations for infants and medical uses in 1946.

As to industrial equipment, South Daito Island has a modern sugar
refinary with a daily capacity of 500 metric tons, together with its accen-
sory installations. On North Daito Island there exist fve old-style work-
places for the manufacture of brown sugar.

11. Marcus Island (Minami-Tori-ohima).
1. Geography.
Marcus Island is situated at 24°17'30" N. and 158°58' E., 684 mle
east-south-east of Chichi-jima of the Ogasawara Island Group.. It is
isolated oceanic island, formed of coral reefs, flat and nearly triangular
in shape, and about 0.8 square miles in area, its highest point being onty
19 meters above sea level.
The island is of much importance for meteorological observations,
especially for typhoons.
2. History.
It seems that existence o7 the island was first reported in 1864 by an
American missionary who called it "Marcus" or "Weeks". Subsequently,
it was visited by an American ship in 1874 and by a French ship in 1880.
The island then came to be inserted in hydrographic charts, but it was
at that time uninhabited and its ownership was uncertain.
In 1879, a Japanese called SAITO, Seizaemon, and others, while sailing
in these waters, found the island, and by visiting it several times, thereafter
ascertained that it was not inhabited. In 1896 one MIZUTANI, Shinroku,
came to settle on the island with 28 workers under him and to engage in
catching birds and fish.
On July 24, 1898, the Japanese Government made the island a part
of Tokyo Prefecture and placed it under the jurisdiction of its Ogasaware
Branch Offce and granted licence to Misutani to carry on his undertaking.
Previously to the above, an American called A. Rosehill, cruising the
south seas in 1889, discovered the island, and finding it uninhabited, afixed
an American flag to a coco-palm. After returning to Honolulu, he applied
to the United States State Department for permission to take guanos of
the island. But, as he did not go through requlsite formalities, no action
was'taken, his application being only kept on record. In 1902-the Japa-
nese Government learned that the aforesaid Rosehill had formed a arm
called the Marcus Ialand Guano Company and was coming to the leland.
In order to explaim to him the fact that the laland was ofmclally lack
in Japam's territory in 1898, the Japanese Government dees

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.98)
spot Foreign Ministry Secretary ISHll, Kikujiro (subeequeatly Vi
arrived at the island on the 27th of the same month.
Rosehill was not there as yet. So, a small corps headed by Loute-
nant Akimoto remained on the island, and the Kusagi sailed homoward.
Secretary Ishii left a letter addressed to Rosehill stating the status of the
island and suggesting that if there were any doubt as to which country
the island belonged to, it should be discussed between the American and
Japanese Governments and the individuals should abide by their decision.
A day after the Kasagi left the island, Captain Rosehill arrived. He read
the letter handed by Lieutenant Akimoto and salled back to Honolulu
after two or three days. On his return Rosehill applied to the United
States Government for the payment by the Japanese Government of the
damages incurred by the Marcus Island Guano Company and of his ex-
pedition expenses. The United States Government did not, however,
entertain the application.
No question has since arisen between Japan and the United States
concerning the island.
3. Inhabitants.
In 1902 there were 29 inhabitants on the island, engaged in catching
birds and making them into stuffed specimens. Their number fluctuated
between 30 and 50. Later, a firm called Minami Tori Shima Company
had been carrying on the mining of guanos, cultivation of coco-palms,
and flishing. But since November, 1930, most of the inhabitants have
quit the island.
IIl. Parece Vela Island (Oki-no-Tori-shima).
1. Grography.
Situated at 20°25' N. and 136°5' E. about 370 miles southwest of
Minami-Iwo-jima, Parece Vela Island is an isolated uninhabited oceanic
atoll without outlet. It is oblong in form, about 2.5 miles from east to
west and from half a mile to 1 mile from south to north.
With the exception of one or two above-water rocks on the western
side the atoll is usually submerged, with rocks appearing here and there
at low tides.
2. History.
In the Spanish charts published up to 16s9, there was mentioned
"Parece Vela," which corresponds to the above island. But it is not clear
when it was discovered and who discovered it. Probably the island was
(6)

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
found before the beginning of the seventeemth
who frequented the Pacific Ocean between
Islands.
In 1689 this island was sighted by Dutchmen.
Janszoon Tasman, and called by them as Engelsbroogte. Thie
then, the name Parece Vela being still employed. In 1789 English Captain
William Douglas visited the island and since then it has come to be pall
Douglas Reef. Further, in 1890 the reef was surveyed by the B
vessel Amphion.
The Japanese Government despatched the warship Monshs to the
atoll twice, in 1922 and in 1925. In the chart published in Japan, in May.
1922, it was noted as Parece Vela, but in the one published in Septemher,
1929, it was put down as Oki-no-Tori.
On July 6, 1931, it was incorporated into the Japanese territory and
placed under the jurisdiction of the Ogasawara Branch Ofce of Tokyo
Prefecture.
This island constitutes an important outpost in connection with the
chservation of typhoons. It is also so situated as to make it an land
mark on the air and sea routes. It was therefore decided to set up on t
a navigation beacon and a meteorological observatory.
The Japanese Government began construction work in June, 1986.
It was necessary to build up the foundation upon the coral reef under
water. Because of the diffculty of transporting materials to this far and
isolated island, and the absence in the vicinity of any convenient anchor-
age for shelter from typhoons, the construction proved an onerous task.
The project was dropped after three years chiefly spent im the com-
pletion of the foundation.

Chapter 11. Minor Islands in the Jappn Sea
Introduction.
Of the minor islands in the Japan Sea, the present chapter deals
the Liancourt Rocks and Dagelet Island, which lle off the Tsushima str
and some 50 miles apart from each other.
The Liancourt Rocks, also called Hornet Islands in Europe, is
in Japan as Take-shima. The Japanese name for Dagelet is Mstsu-st
or Utsuryo, of which the Korean equivalent Ul-lung is also used som
times in the West.
The existence of these islands was known to Japanese in early times
(Note 1). Originally it was the Liancourts which were called "Matsu
shima", and Dagelet which was called "Take-shima". The revesing of
the nanmes originated in a curious combination of circumstance.
The old-time "Take-shima" was visited by Europeans in the begin-
ning of the 18th century—firstly by Captain de La Pérouse (Note 2) and
let" and "Argonaut". But as the two explorers had given different longl-
tudinal measurements, "Take-shima" was shown as two islands in the
maps then made in Europe.
Philip Franz von Siebold, when preparing his map of Japan published
in 1840, thought that the two islands long known to Japanese as Matsu-
shima and Take-shima corresponded to Dagelet and Argonaut; and he
designated Dagelet, supposed to be in the east, as Matsu-shima, and Ar-
gonaut, supposed to be in the west, as Take-shima.
Afterwards, although it was established that the two islands were
in reality one and Argonaut was discarded, Dagelet continued to be called
Matsu-shima, and the name Take-shima came to be transferred to the
Island which had been originally Matsu-shima and which was later named
Note 1. Professor TAROHAsHI, Kiyoshi, writes in his monograph Utsuryo Island-its
discovery and its possession (Seikyu-Gakuho No. 3, Fobrumry 1981):
It is in more or less ancient times that Japanese fishermen in the fan-in.
district had come to gain a fair knowledge of these ialands and it is gizite
possible that in the days of early history a ses route was alreaedy knowm to
reach Kores from the San-in district through Qki Islands, Take-shime and
Utsuryo Island.
Note 3.
Captain Jean Francois de La Perouse, under the osders of King Louis XVI of
France, left France in 1785 in command of two frigates Boussole and Astrolebe
and, on discovering Utsuryo Island on May 97, 1787, named it " Dagelet." The
nase was taken after professor Lepante Dagalet of l'Ecole Militaire on bonsd the
Boussole who firmt sighted the island.

Ldameourts by the Freach.
I. Liancourt Rooks (Take-shima).
1. Geography.
Liancourt Rocks are situated at 37°9' N. and 131'56' E, being about
86 miles from Oki Islands of Shimane Prefecture. They consist of a pair
of islets, 0.06 and 0.02 square miles respectively and a number of rocks
scattered around them. The islets, composed of tarren rocks and dovold
of any overgrowth, look white being covered with birds' droppings. The
rugged beaches dotted with strange looking caves are noted as breeding
grounds of sea-lions (zalophus lobatus). Lacking in open spaces and
drinking water, the islets are unfit for human habitation. The rock
scattered around are generally flat at their tops and barely show them-
sclves above water.
2. History.
As stated in the Introduction, the Jepanese knew the exiitence of
the Liancourt Rocks from the anclent times. Bút the earliest document-
ary evidence of this knowledge is to be found in the Inshu Shicho Göki
(Oki Provnce; Things Seen and Heard) a book published in 1667, which
contains the following description:
To the northwest from the Province of Oki there is Matsu-shima
at a two days' distance, and at another day's distance further out
there is Take-shima. The latter, also called Iso-take-shima, is rich,
in bamboo, fish etc.
It is clear that Matsu-shima here refers to the Liancourts (Illustra-
tion).
As for European acquaintance with the Rocks, it was in 1849 that
the Liancourt, a French whaling ship, first sighted them and gave them
their present name. The Pallada, a Russian frigate under the command
of Admiral Putiatin, is said to have taken soundings of the adjacent sea
in 1854. In the following year came the Hornet, a corvette of the British
China Fleet, which also sounded the vicinity of the Rocks.
It should be noted that while there is a Korean name for Dagelet,
none exists for the Liancourts Rocks and they are not shown in the maps
made in Korea.
On February 22, 1905, the Governor of Shimane Prefecture, by a
prefectural proclamation, placed the Liancourts under the jurisdictiop of
the Oki Islands Branch Offce of the Shimane Prefectural government
(Note 8).
Note 8. The United States Hydrographicel Surocy a   with 
under the head of Oki group of inlaarda.

II. Dagelet Island (Matsu-shima, Utsuryo or Ul-lung Island).
1. Geography.
Dagelet Island is situated at 37'30'N. and 130'52'E. and a
equidistant from the port of Fushan of Korea and the port of Sak
Tottori Prefecture, Japan. Its area is 28 square miles. Almost
covered with volcanic rocks, it presents a complicated topography
a lone peak in the middle, rising 983 meters above sea level.
The terrain is rugged, surrounded by steep cliffs nearly all aro
and there is no good anchorage in the adjacent waters. The mount
areas were fornmerly covered with dense woods but, owing to reck
deforestation, there remain to-day only a few aged trees here and th
at higher altitudes. The flora of this island, though forming a smiall
distinct domain, is recognized to have many common features with Jap
Proper. For instance, while 14 out of 90 specimens of trees are endemle.
64 are identical with those of the central part of Honshu. In passing, It
is interesting to note that the cultivation in Japan of the Take-shime
lily (lilium hansonii), an endemic plant of the island, is mentioned in a
book published in 1710, a fact which points to an early intercourse be-
tween the island and Japan.
2. History.
It is only in 1787 that the island was first mentioned, by the name
of Dagelet, in the world history of discoveries by Captain Jean. François
de La Pérouse of the French navy (See Note 2). In Japanese documents,
however, reference was made as early as 1004 to Uruma Island, an old
Japanese equivalent for Ul-lung Island.
The island was known to Koreans also from ancient times. Several
attempts at colonization were made by Koreans after the middle of the
12th century. The Island became later a convenient hiding-place by
criminals and brigands. The Korean Government from 1400 and on-
ward adhered for a long time to a policy of keeping it uninhabited.
As the island was thus virtually abandoned by the Korean Govern-
ment, the Japanese continued to frequent it in increasing numbers. The
expedition of TOYOTOMi, Hideyoshi to Korea in 1592 served to accelerate
activities of the Japanese in this area, and for about, a century thereafter
(10)

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.97)
the lsland remained in all appearasces a Japane
From the beginning of the 17th century rep
carried on between Japan and Koren on the question of
island.
In 1692 the arrival of a large númber of Koresns in the letand
rise to a dispute, on which negotiations were conducted between
Korean Government and the feudal lord of Tsushima representing th
Shogunate. As a result, in 1697 the Tokugawa Shogunate py
the Japanese from going to the island for fishing and informed the t
Government to that effect. The question was thus apparently settled for
the time being.
The Korean authorities, however, made no change in its polley to
keep the island uninhabited even after the above mentloned incident.
They despatched officials only once in three years to have them cut down
trees and bamboos and collect native products to be presented to the
government. The Japanese, therefore, never stopped fishing near the
island (Note 5).
Toward the end of the Tokugawa Shogunate and in the early years
of Meiji (around 1868), a movement gathered force in Japan urging the
development of "Matsu-shima" and petitions were made to the Goverm-
ment. As it was ascertained that "Matsu-shima" was none other than
the island which was once the subject of negotiations with the Korean
Government and which the Japanese had been prohibited from entering.
the petitions were not taken up by the Japanese Government, although
Japanese still continued to go to the island. On the other hand the Ko-
rean Government made repeated demands to Japan to keep her subjects
out of the island and at the samè time tried to develop the ialand them
selves but with no appreciable results.
The Annexation of Korea in 1910 brought the island under the rule
of the Government-General of Chosen.

formation

2.
Historic Sitos are those areas or structures deemed by the pert
of the Ministry of Education to be worthy of preservation because of
their historic significance, and often relate to members of the Imperial
family.
Scenic Spots aro those localities vhich have been deemad partie
ularly beaut:1l.  which aro celebrated in Japanese musio or litere-
turc.
Nat l istory Proserves aro those designated areas in which are
plants, nimals, or mineralogical spacimons and formations are found.
In some casos the tem is applied to certain protected animals, as in
the case o the Japanese antelope. When this happens, the area listed
is usually designatad as uncortain or indefinite.
A combination of two or all three types of areas is often found
in one locality, but for the purpose of this report. the main claasi-
fication :s usad.

SUBJECT: Request for Arrangement of Lands Between Korea and Japem
TO:
Gen. Douglas MacArthur, Supreme Commander, SCAP
We, Koream people, are ever so interesting for your negotiation
for peace to Japan, because Korea stands near by Japan and Korea was
under the cruel compression of Japan for years and vast sacrifices
were always paid to Japan. We expect you that so sharp and keen
plans for establishment of oriental peace and order and are waiting
for its announcement. Korea who has no actual voice in the confer-
ence should be wall considered by your great plans.
Speaking oriental peace, Korean opinions which will play a great
role in it, is absolutely necessary. Before some advices are sub-
mitted you in the course of negotiation of peace and establishment
or oriental orders, we, Patriotic Oldmen's Association, are to smb-
mit a request on arrangment of lands, believing your kind consider-
ation.
Importance of legal arrangement of lands in the international
order, was shown in the corridor at the east Europe established is
the Versailles construction.
Generally speaking, legal arrangemental standards stand on a
passive side, e.g., restoration of lands taken away, and on the other
active side, e.g., division of land for supporting development some
nation and for the peace of the nation.
On the land matters between Korea and Japan, both active and
pessive sides are present and we herety request as follows:
I. Returning back the island "Docksm."
It is not suspected at all but well understood that the
island "Ulneumgde", and its attached are belenging to Koree histori-
cally and astually. Japan, hewever, plammed to profit by fishing
and feresting wader the eveemsting poliey or Kores fer intereal
seourity prohibiting people to ester the aland. Armed rebbery-
féabers of West Ceast of Japan dared toavede into the ialand and
assert that the island was belenging to Japen and wedieposed en-
tanglement ccntimeed. Bet in ascordame with the reselute megotie-

both countries, Korea and Japan, became to arise, again. Under the
