and his advisor G. von Mollendorf, Japan surrendered to Korea, and
thereafter Korean Govemment manmged in legaily and absolutely, and
no chance was given them to re-make such difficulties. On the other
hand, due to the Russian racific Policy, international equality was
made and finally Japan closed to invade somewhere.
Japan, however, never dismiss the fishering profit around the
island "Ulnoungdo", but planned to occupy a corner of it by some
means and became to find out a small island calle: "Docksung" in
Korean name, meaning a small pot-shaped island, ne r the Ulnoungdo,
where whales gathered. In 1904, Japanese fisher of Tottoriken named
Nakai Yosaburo made a cruel programme to seize th: Docksum and was
practised to submit petitions or requests to the ydrographic depart-
ment of Navy, Dep't of Home Affairs, Dep't of Foreign Affairs and
Dep't of Agriculture and Commerce, etc. to register the island into
Japanese territory.
At that time, considering the delicate international relations,
Japanese officials hesitated to put into practice. Japan, however,
guessed the Russian failure in the Russian-Japanese War, and Pro-
vincial Notice No. 40 of Tottoriken was issued officially as follows:
"Islands located 85 miles far from Okishima are hereby called
Takeshima and transferred to this province." Thus, these ialands
were occupied by Japanese Government. This was of so secrecy that
not only Korean Govermment but also either nations could not know it.
Furthermore, even if Korean Government turned his attention to it,
no policy might be made for it. As above, Japanese wills were com-
pletely successful on it.
The so-called Takeshima is the very "Dockusum (sic) in Korean
name," and this is nmely, Liancourt Rocks on the world's chart.
This name "Liancourt-Rocks" was established due to the name of the
French whaler which found out the island. Thereafter, by the Russian
Warship "Palleada" in 1854 and by the English warship "Hornet" in
1855, the said island was re-found and the ship's name was given to
it. But it was never suspected that these variously named island
was the very island "Ulneungdo."
In the 2nd edition of "the Korean Hydrographies" published by
the Japanese Navy Hydrographic Department 1889, the Liancourt-Rocks
and Ulneungdo are described as a sme island. It is to be consider-
ably said that a Japanese district office carried out such a plan
imposing upon the world. As a matter of course, it is well under-
stood that such an ialand occupied illegally by Japan should be
returned to Korea.
II. Transferring the island "Tsushima" to Korea.
The island "Tsushima" located at the boundary between the
Sea of Kores and the Sea of Japan is a strategic point on the trems-
portation from Japan to the Continent. Its geographical importance
is easily recomised by the defest of Russia. We have to waderstand
that

Oriental-nistorically, the Tsushima was the Waiku's headquarters
of aggression into the orient for 5 centuries since the 5th century,
and Korean-nistorically, it was the den of the burglars who abused
their power in the front of horea for about 2,000 years.
Thus, Japan always marched out into Korea or the continent by
using the island as a basal point or as a stepping point. In the
world-map made by Lapo Homem, 1554 (an old oriental map) the

Tsushima and its character was already known among the Europeans.
Escaping from the attacking of the pirates based at the Tsushime,

pieces of cloth and other mans of livings annually, for 5 centuries.
when we consider that black clouds covering the recent Korean history
started from Japan and the ill air current was retained in this
island, Korean people cannot pacify their nerves against the Tsushima.
In the local reconstruction after the great world war II, the
Tsushima should be transferred to Korea as to
1) remove absolutely a standing menace to the livings of Korean
people,
2) prohibit the Japanese aggressive emergence into the continent,
3) prevent the trampling upon the orient of the burglars.
We hereby indicate the appropriate basal conditions as follows:
a. Geographically: The localization of the Tsushima is rather
nearer to Korea than to Japan, and it is not unnaturally thought that
the Tsushima belongs to Korea if we dicide its belonging freely,
standing far from the political conditions.
b. Historically: The name "Tsushima" was originated from the

two islands actually) and the grounding civilisation of the Tsushime
were almostly due to the Korean old civilisation, as many scholars
said, and it is well indicated that the recent civilisation was made
by the hand of the Japanese of Korean origin, according to various
references.
First of all, when Korea subjugated the Tsushima 1396 and 1419,
due to their bad faith, the Tsushime re-recognised the relation be-
tween both nations, namely, master and servant. And as most of the
people in the Tsushima received official ranks and salaries from
Korean Government, Korea thought the Tsushima a land belonging to
Koreen Government, and this fact was clearly deseribed in the history
and geography of Kores (Lee's Korea). It is explained that Teashime
belonged to Korea md then to Korea and Japan sinse Japanese pirates
occupied the island. Such instances occupied by two natioms, are

c. Politically: Though the oriental peace will be completely

Thereafter, when the Meiji-restoration was established and

fishing treaty. This fishing profit obtained in the Korean coast
which center is the Korean channel, is necessary for only the island

It is doubtlessly expected that the fighting on their fishing

UICLASSIFIED
IMPRESSIONS OF HIROSHIMA
I spent three days in Hiroshina from March 30 to April 2, 1949
while on leave from the Office of the Political Adviser. Arrenge-
ments for my wife and me to visit Hiroshima were made by Mr. Takiso
MATSUMOTO, People's Cooperative Party member of the House of Repre-
sentatives of the Diet representing Hiroshima.
We arrived in Hiroshima at 3:30 in the afternoon of March 30.
having left Tokyo the preceding evening, and took a brief ride about
the city. I was immediately impressed by the extent of rebailding.
Most of the area about the station, itself a large building rebuilt
since the war, was entirely occupied by small shops, houses, or
corrugated iron shacks. Two days earlier a large fire had destroyed
was in sharp contrast with the buildings around it. Although I had
never been in Hiroshime before, I had expected evidence of war damage
have been completely built up came as a surprise. Cae buildima
has been left untouched as a symbol of the destruction caused by th
atomic bomb, and the dilapidated condition of this large building.
particularly its dome-shaped roof, stands out. Buildings of rein-
forced concrete construction appear generally to have withstood the
blast, although their interiors were without exception severely
damaged.
It was soon obrious, however, that most of the reconstructiom
was of an insubstantial wooden type characteristic of practically
all building in Japan since the end of the war. This type of con-
struction was all the more noticeable and depressing in Hiroshime
because of its complete prevalence. Much of the city appeareas
if it had been hastily built up in a very short time. Some a the
streets downtown were very narrow and buildings were jammed ekt to
each other, all evidently repeating the prewar pattern. Im contrast
were many open spaces, some consisting of only a single plot of land
and others as much as a htndred yards square, which have net yet bees
rebuilt: some of the larger areas were being held open, we were teld,
as a result of city plans for sohools and civic buildings. The rab-
ble for the most part has been oleared away.
Hiroshime is built in the delta of the Ota River, whieh. remifies
into seven branches as it empties into the Inland Sea. Surrounding
the city on the other three sides are a series of hills. The delta
is about thirteen square miles in area. It wes this aree which was
devastated by the concussion, radiation, and fires caused by the
atemic bomb. Buildings on the hills around the city, mestly resi-
totd thes ther vere deverely shakes but sot otherviseà

No. 851, Apri1 23, 1949.
The resort and shrine at Itsukushime about twenty miles from s
famous as one of Japan's three beautiful sites, were of ceurse
and greatly relieve the somewhat oppressive atmosphere of Mireshime
Also located well apart from the center of the damage is the Tave
Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha (Far East Industrial), which is the primespel
pany is one of Japan's chief producers of sanrinsha, three-wheeled ve
hicles resembling motorcycles with a small carrying capacity; it pee
duces about 500 of these vehicles every month and also mamufactares
drills used for mining coal. During the war the company made rifles
in addition to its present products, and as a result a part af its
ment remains in the factory but is not being used. The company make
all parts of its vehicles except the rubber accessories, and gives
the impression of being extremely well-operated. Mr. Matsuda, a self
made man who received little schooling and built the company entisely
crease produstisa if it hat the material. Me. Maté
and that if possible he could retain at least part of the machimery
now set aside for reparations. I was unable to learn much about Mr
Matsuda's labor policy; I had heard that he had had labor treubles

difficulty and that the labor union was a company organisation. e
pointed out that the company has a clinic, recreation rooms and

that the office and factory appeared to be clean and well-lighted.
All in all, one could not help being impressed with this cempasy as
a model of medium-sised industry in Japan.
We also spent peveral hours visiting the buildings of the Atemie

Kure. Doctor Tessmer stated thet they are receiving excellent ee-

having some trouble combating the mistaken impression that the Com-

the Commission is renlly just beginning its work, but that to date
there is no indication that the bomb created abnormal genetic
effects. He cautioned that it was premature to draw any significant
conclusions in regard to the long-term effect of the atomie bomb.
Japanese residents of Hiroshina were without exception villing
to discuss the atomic bomb, although several said that it was no
longer an ordinary topic of discussion among themselves. All whe
were in Hiroshima at the time could remember vividly where they were
when the bomb burst at eight o'clock on the morning of August 6. 1945.
Few of them were left untouched by the blas:, radiation, or fire thet
followed. Almost every family lost several members. It was diffieult
to comprehend the fatalism of a man who lost his wife and four chil-
dren and said he had closed off that part of his life. The Mayor of
Hiroshime told us that although the off icial number of pereems killen
inclmde the figures for mfss ing and-for matitery paroosh

to about 100.000.
We talked at length with two Catholic priests who had lived in
with Father KLEINSoRGE, whose experience was described in John HaY!
Hiroshime -- at the time of the explosion. They have already commemeed
buildins on their former mite and have plans for a large church tomke
atarted in the near future. The priests said that the number of t
parishioners has increased somewhat over prewar figures but that there
has been no great influx of converts to any of the Christian churches
in Hiroshima.
We spent the evening before our departure talking with the Mayor
of Hiroshima, Mr. Shinso HamaI, who told us of some of the diffioulties
the city is facing. Chief of these is the problem of financing recom-
struction. Mr. Hamai said that the city has received liberal rants
from the Mational Diet but that in view of recent budgetary restrictiems
these allotments are likely to be greatly reduced or oliminated. He
pointed to the lack of an effective sewage system and of anything like
a sufficient mumber of schools as primary handicaps, and said that
carefal city planning is in danger of defeat because of the city's
imability to construct the buildings and roads it desires. As evidence
of rehabilitation he pointed to the large number of dwelling units e
in the eity, which new houses with reasonable adequacy about 240, 000
people: Hiroshima's wartime population was 450,000. The supply ef
feod, water, and alectrieity is good: transportation, including that

DATE: Ootober 31, 1949
FROM : L/P - Conrad E. Snow 
EPARTMENT OF STATE
SUBJECT:
Attached letter from Eastern United Company, Inc. regarding
the term "territory of Japan" and whether this includes the
island of Okinawa
It seems to m- that the Eastern United Company, Inc., should
be advised that the Department of State cannot assume to interpret
the words "territory of Japan" in the instrument in question without
having the instrument before it. They might be advised that Okinawe
was recognized internationally as Japanese territory before the war
and that no trensfer of Japanese territory has taken place. You
might add, of course, that it is occupied by the United States and
will not be returned to Japan upon conclusion of a peace treaty.

Gentlemen:
Among other agencies, we hold exclusive franchise for irform Cons
truction for the Far East including the territory of Japan.
There exists some uncertainty as to whether such term as "territory
of Japan" includes the island of Okinawa.
It is our belief that until after peace treaty with Japan has been
signed, legally the ieland of Okinawa constitutes part of Japan and conse-
quently is included in our contract under the term of "territory of Japan".
Any clarification we can receive from you in this connection will enable
us to settle this point with our principals, Airform Construction Co. in Los
Angeles, and needless to mention will be greatly appreciated by us.
Yours very truly
Blu
